<!--
Hi everyone! I’m Josh – I’m a full time independent open source maintainer. I focus on static analysis tooling around JavaScript & TypeScript – most notably, typescript-eslint, the tooling that allows you to run ESLint and Prettier on TypeScript code. 
I’m also the author of the recent Learning TypeScript book, published by O’Reilly.
-->

<!--
JavaScript is a programming language. It provides a standard set of syntax to describe values and how to use those values: variables, string literals, assignments, and so on. 
The big problem with JavaScript is that it doesn’t have a built-in, standardized way to describe the intent behind those values. This code snippet shows creating a value variable, then assigning it first a string, then a number. There’s no way in JavaScript to say, hey, I want this value to hold a string.
-->

transition: { name: view-transition, duration: 500 }

<!--
The TypeScript programming language looks a lot like JavScript.
TypeScript adds in some new syntax to the world of JavaScript. Here we see a ‘type annotation’ indicating that our value variable should only ever be a string.
-->

</div>

<!--
There is also a program called the TypeScript type checker that can run on your code and tell you if there are any mismatches between the way code constructs such as variables were initialized and how they’re used.
Here, we see the TypeScript command-line, ”tsc”, telling us that an index.ts file mismatches in its third line of code. We said that our value variable is a string, but then gave it a number. What gives?!
-->

transition: { name: fade, duration: 350 }

<!--
Lastly, TypeScript also provides nice integrations for editors such as VS Code, called language services. Editors can continuously run TypeScript’s language services to help you during development. If you’re already a TypeScript developer you’re probably intimately familiar with the “red squiggly”. Red squigglies are provided by language services to tell you that, if you hover your mouse over the squigglied code, you should read the TypeScript complaint for it.
-->

---
layout: none
hide: true
transition: { name: fade, duration: 500 }
---

<div class="pptx-shape pptx-text" style="height:104.38px;left:0px;top:0px;width:960px;justify-content:center;padding-bottom:3.6px;padding-left:7.2px;padding-right:7.2px;padding-top:3.6px"><p style="font-family:'League Spartan';font-size:49px;font-weight:700;line-height:52.92px;text-align:center"><span style="color:#FFFFFF;font-family:'League Spartan';font-size:49px;font-weight:700">The TypeScript Type Cycle</span></p></div>
<svg class="pptx-shape" xmlns="http://www.w3.org/2000/svg" viewBox="26.06 154.01 918.01 367.58" style="height:367.58px;left:26.06px;top:154.01px;width:918.01px"><path d="M72 475C135.3 398.3 173 217.9 235.9 201.1C298.8 184.3 379.8 360 449.5 374.2C519.2 388.4 576.2 303.4 654.3 286.6C732.4 269.7 820.3 276.7 898 274.2" fill="none" stroke="rgb(200,215,250)" stroke-width="9.5" /></svg>
</div>

<!--
Many developers go through a variant of the Gartner hype cycle after they first master TypeScript types.
In the peak of inflated expectations, they feel great about themselves -- as they should! They mastered the type system; all sorts of wild and wacky features like generics and conditional types are at their disposal! TypeScript!!
But, those wild and wacky types come at a cost. They’re harder to write, harder to read, and much harder to debug. Turns out many of the generics and conditional types they used at the peak were unnecessary and over-inflated code complexity. Thus we exit the Peak of Inflated Types and enter the Trough of Type Disillusionment.
But, fear not: we all learn from our mistakes. That’s what it means to be a developer. Having learned the hard way not to overuse TypeScript’s type system features, developers enter the third phase in their TypeScript journey: the Plateau of Productivity. It’s here that TypeScript shines the best.
-->

</div>

<!--
But wait, haven’t we been talking about linting this whole time? Doesn’t TypeScript lint my code? Isn’t TypeScript a linter?
That’s a very reasonable question! The answer is: somewhat no. On the surface, TypeScript can feel like it’s “linting” your code for small mistakes. But the design and functionality of a linter is very different from a type checker.
-->

Type Checkers 🧠</span></p></div></div>
</div>

<!--
Linters and type checkers fall into a category of tools called “static analysis”, along with a third common tool, the formatter. As with any classification of tool, there are a few different tradeoffs between the different forms of static analysis. We’ve typically consolidated into three types on a spectrum from fast to powerful.
The fastest is formatters such as Prettier. They run very quickly on your code and don’t touch program logic – they just make the code look pretty. More on that later.
The most powerful are type checkers such as TypeScript. They run more slowly and build up a full understanding of your entire application. More on that later.
In the middle are linters such as ESLint. They typically run on a file-by-file basis, which makes them faster than type checkers. But they still work with program logic, which makes them slower than formatters. More on that later.
One commonality between all forms of static analysis is that all these tools need a way to represent the code they’re working with. They need a standardized format to describe the contents of your source files and other code texts.
-->

layout: none
transition: { name: fade, duration: 500 }
---

<!--
For example, one of my favorite rules that ships with ESLint is logical-assignment-operators. It’s one of many “syntax protip” rules that lets you know when there’s a better syntax for what you’re trying to do. In the case of this code:
friend = friend || ”me”;
…fun fact, there’s actually a new JavaScript “logical assignment operator” that does the same thing in fewer characters:
friend ||= ”me”;
The ESLint rule would look at the former, let you know there’s a better way, and -when prompted to- auto-fix to the latter.
A type checker would never tell you to make this switch. The code runs just as well after as it did before. The linter’s goal is not to enforce
-->

layout: none
transition: { name: fade, duration: 500 }
---

<!--
The prefer-as-const rule lets you know when there’s a more succinct way to write a literal type assertion. It’s ok if you’re not familiar with this particular TypeScript syntax point: just know that an ‘as’ assertion like this one can be rewritten with a cleaner syntax. Just like the ||= logical assignment operator from before, this rule can auto-fix your code to be nicer.
-->

---
layout: none
transition: { name: fade, duration: 500 }
---

<div class="pptx" style="">
<div class="pptx-shape pptx-text" style="height:253.45px;left:101.71px;top:118.45px;width:756.57px;justify-content:center;padding-bottom:3.6px;padding-left:7.2px;padding-right:7.2px;padding-top:3.6px"><p style="font-family:'Regular Bold';font-size:35px;font-weight:700;line-height:42px;text-align:center"><span style="color:#C8D7FA;font-family:'Regular Bold';font-size:35px;font-weight:700">Syntax </span><span style="color:#C8D7FA;font-family:'Regular Bold';font-size:35px;font-weight:700">!==</span><span style="color:#C8D7FA;font-family:'Regular Bold';font-size:35px;font-weight:700"> Types</span></p></div>
</div>

<!--
Let’s take a step back for a moment. I mentioned earlier that TypeScript provides deep insights to your code with type checking. But this TypeScript-specific prefer-as-const rule didn’t use any of that. It’s just a syntax rule. Not types.
Types are different from syntax. ASTs only store the syntax seen in your file. They don’t store the deeper type information that TypeScript would know about. For example, you might have a ‘console’ identifier in code – but knowing that it has methods such as ‘log’ and ‘info’ wouldn’t be known just from the AST.
-->

transition: { name: fade, duration: 500 }

<!--
One of our type-checked rules, await-thenable, lets you know if you have an ‘await’ in front of something that doesn’t need to be awaited. Fun fact about JavaScript: ‘await’ directly gives back the value it’s provided if that value isn’t a Thenable (meaning, an object with a .then, such as a Promise).
await-thenable can let you know about an unnecessary await – and can suggest removing it for you.
This example is a little contrived (I’ve never seen someone await a console log) so let’s look at a bigger one.
-->

---
layout: none
transition: { name: fade, duration: 500 }
---

<div class="pptx" style="">

<!--
This code -written for a frontend framework you may not be familiar with- describes a button with a running state. The button receives an onClick prop. It sets up an internal running boolean for state. When clicked, it sets running to true, awaits the onClick, then sets running to false. Its DOM element is disabled while running.
The ‘await onClick()’ is unnecessary because onClick is typed as a function that returns void (meaning: nothing, ignore the result).
-->

---
layout: none
transition: { name: fade, duration: 500 }
---

<div class="pptx-shape pptx-text" style="height:104.38px;left:0px;top:0px;width:960px;justify-content:center;padding-bottom:3.6px;padding-left:7.2px;padding-right:7.2px;padding-top:3.6px"><p style="font-family:'League Spartan';font-size:49px;font-weight:700;line-height:52.92px;text-align:center"><span style="color:#FFFFFF;font-family:'League Spartan';font-size:49px;font-weight:700">The TypeScript Type Cycle</span></p></div>
<svg class="pptx-shape" xmlns="http://www.w3.org/2000/svg" viewBox="26.06 154.01 918.01 367.58" style="height:367.58px;left:26.06px;top:154.01px;width:918.01px"><path d="M72 475C135.3 398.3 173 217.9 235.9 201.1C298.8 184.3 379.8 360 449.5 374.2C519.2 388.4 576.2 303.4 654.3 286.6C732.4 269.7 820.3 276.7 898 274.2" fill="none" stroke="rgb(200,215,250)" stroke-width="9.5" /></svg>
</div>

<!--
Many developers go through a variant of the Gartner hype cycle after they first master TypeScript types.
In the peak of inflated expectations, they feel great about themselves -- as they should! They mastered the type system; all sorts of wild and wacky features like generics and conditional types are at their disposal! TypeScript!!
But, those wild and wacky types come at a cost. They’re harder to write, harder to read, and much harder to debug. Turns out many of the generics and conditional types they used at the peak were unnecessary and over-inflated code complexity. Thus we exit the Peak of Inflated Types and enter the Trough of Type Disillusionment.
But, fear not: we all learn from our mistakes. That’s what it means to be a developer. Having learned the hard way not to overuse TypeScript’s type system features, developers enter the third phase in their TypeScript journey: the Plateau of Productivity. It’s here that TypeScript shines the best.
-->
